<!-- lemma-15: body_md -->
(\textit{cf.}~\cite[Theorems 4.1 and 5.1]{Agrawal2004Annals})\label{lem_primetest}
    There is an algorithm which can decide, for any given integer $u\ge2$, whether $u$ is a prime in $(\log u)^{O(1)}$ bit operations.
<!-- end lemma-15 -->
<!-- lemma-16: body_md -->
\label{lem_gm}
For every $\bmb\in\Sigma_q^m$, there exists
$\alpha\in\mathbb{F}_{P_m}$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq_hbnehc}
 h_{\alpha}(\bmb)\ne h_{\alpha}(\bmc)
\end{equation}
for all $\bmc\in\cB_{k}(\bmb)\setminus\{\bmb\}$. Let $\alpha(\bmb)$ be the smallest $\alpha$ in $\{0,1,\ldots,P_m-1\}$ satisfying this property. Define
$$
 g_m(\bmb)=\alpha(\bmb) P_m+
 h_{\alpha(\bmb)}(\bmb)\in[0,P_m^2-1].
$$ 
Then $g_m(\bmc)\ne g_m(\bmb)$ for every
$\bmc\in\cB_k(\bmb)\setminus\{\bmb\}$. The value $g_m(\bmb)$ is computable in $O_{q,k}(m^6(\log m)^{O(1)})$ bit operations.
<!-- end lemma-16 -->
<!-- ieeeproof-20: body_md -->
For $\bmc=c_1\cdots c_m\in\cB_{k}(\bmb)\setminus\{\bmb\}$, consider the polynomial
$$
 P_{\bmb,\bmc}(X)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}(b_i-c_i)X^{i-1}
 \in\mathbb{F}_{P_m}[X].
$$
Since $\bmb\ne\bmc$, there is at least one $i\in[m]$ such that $b_i\ne c_i$. It follows from the fact $P_m>q-1$ and $0< \left|b_i-c_i\right|\le q-1$ that $P_{\bmb,\bmc}(X)$ is a nonzero polynomial over $\mathbb{F}_{P_m}$. The degree of $P_{\bmb,\bmc}(X)$ is at most $m-1$, implying that it has at most $m-1$ roots. Then by \Cref{lem_confusionball} and the fact $P_m>(D_m-1)(m-1)$, there is at least one element $\alpha\in\mathbb{F}_{P_m}$ such that $P_{\bmb,\bmc}(\alpha)\ne0$ for every $\bmc\in\cB_{k}(\bmb)\setminus\{\bmb\}$.

Suppose that there is some $\bmc\in\cB_{k}(\bmb)\setminus\{\bmb\}$ satisfying $g_m(\bmc)=g_m(\bmb)$. Then it must hold that $\alpha(\bmc)=\alpha(\bmb)$ and $h_{\alpha(\bmb)}(\bmc)=h_{\alpha(\bmb)}(\bmb)$, since $0\le\alpha(\bmc),\alpha(\bmb),h_{\alpha(\bmb)}(\bmc),h_{\alpha(\bmb)}(\bmb)<P_m$. This contradicts \eqref{eq_hbnehc} and therefore, $g_m(\bmc)\ne g_m(\bmb)$ for all $\bmc\in\cB_{k}(\bmb)\setminus\{\bmb\}$.

Next, we analyze the complexity of computing $g_m(\bmb)$.
First, generate $\cB_k(\bmb)$ and test
$\alpha=0,1,\ldots,P_m-1$ in increasing order.
For each $\alpha$, compute $h_{\alpha}(\bmb)$ and
$h_{\alpha}(\bmc)$ for all $\bmc\in\cB_k(\bmb)\setminus\{\bmb\}$. Stop at the first $\alpha$ for which $h_{\alpha}(\bmc)\ne h_{\alpha}(\bmb)$ for all $\bmc\in\cB_k(\bmb)\setminus\{\bmb\}$.

For given $\alpha$ and $\bmc\in\cB_k(\bmb)\setminus\{\bmb\}$, the value $h_{\alpha}(\bmc)$ can be
computed by Horner's rule (see \cite[Section 5.1]{Higham2002}), which uses $m$ multiplications and $m$ additions in $\mathbb{F}_{P_m}$. Since there are at most $P_m$ choices of $\alpha$ and at most $D_m$ sequences to evaluate for each $\alpha$, the total number of field additions and multiplications is $O(P_mD_mm)=O_{q,k}(m^6)$.

Put $\ell=\lceil\log_2 P_m\rceil$. Recall that elements in $\mathbb{F}_{P_m}$ are represented by integers in $[0,P_m-1]$. Addition modulo $P_m$ takes $O(\ell)$ bit operations, and multiplication followed by reduction modulo $P_m$ takes $O(\ell^2)$ bit operations.
Comparing two field elements takes $O(\ell)$ bit operations. Thus, all evaluations and comparisons require
$O(P_mD_mm\ell^2)=O_{q,k}(m^6(\log m)^2)$ bit operations.

Generating $\cB_k(\bmb)$ costs $O_{q,k}(m^3\log m)$ bit operations by \Cref{lem_confusionball}. The prime search costs $O_{q,k}(m^3(\log m)^{O(1)})$ bit operations, as established above. Once $\alpha(\bmb)$ has been found, $h_{\alpha(\bmb)}(\bmb)$ is already available. Then computing $g_m(\bmb)$ requires only one integer multiplication and one addition, which take $O(\ell^2)$ bit operations. Combining these bounds gives the stated complexity $O_{q,k}(m^6(\log m)^{O(1)})$.
<!-- end ieeeproof-20 -->
<!-- lemma-17: body_md -->
\cite[eq.~(3.13)]{Rosser1962}\label{lem_jthprime}
    It holds that $p_j<j(\ln j+\ln\ln j)$ for every integer $j\ge6$.
<!-- end lemma-17 -->
<!-- lemma-18: body_md -->
\label{lem_localrecovery}
Let $\bmb\in\Sigma_q^m$. 
\begin{enumerate}[$(i)$]
    \item The syndrome $\mathsf{H}_m(\bmb)$ can be computed in $O_{q,k}\left( m^8(\log m)^{O(1)} \right)$ bit operations.
    \item Given $\bmw\in\cE_{1,k}(\bmb)$ and $\mathsf{H}_m(\bmb)$, the sequence $\bmb$ can be uniquely recovered in $O_{q,k}\left( m^7(\log m)^{O(1)} \right)$ bit operations.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-18 -->
<!-- ieeeproof-21: body_md -->
(i) To estimate the complexity of computing $\mathsf{H}_m(\bmb)$, we estimate the complexity of each step in the computation of $\mathsf{H}_m(\bmb)$. The syndrome $\mathsf{H}_m(\bmb)$ can be computed by the following steps:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Step 1}: Generate $\cB_k(\bmb)$ using \Cref{lem_confusionball}. This step takes $O_{q,k}(m^3\log m)$ bit operations.
    \item \textbf{Step 2}: Compute $g_m(\bmc)$ for all $\bmc\in\cB_{k}(\bmb)$, including $\bmc=\bmb$. Since $ \left|\cB_{k}(\bmb)\right|\le D_m=O_{q,k}(m^2)$, it follows from \Cref{lem_gm} that this step takes $O_{q,k}\left( m^8(\log m)^{O(1)} \right)$ bit operations. Moreover, each $g_m(\bmc)$ has $O_{q,k}(\log m)$ bits.
    \item \textbf{Step 3}: For each $\bmc\in\cB_k(\bmb)\setminus\{\bmb\}$, put $\Delta_{\bmc}= \left|g_m(\bmb)-g_m(\bmc)\right|$. Calculating all the differences $\Delta_{\bmc}$ takes $O_{q,k}(m^2\log m)$ bit operations.
    \item \textbf{Step 4}: Generate the first $T_m$ primes. To that end, test the integers $2,3,\ldots$ in order and retain those that are prime, stopping when $T_m$ primes have been obtained. By \Cref{lem_jthprime} and the bound $T_m=O_{q,k}(m^2\log m)$, we have $p_{T_m}=O_{q,k}(m^2(\log m)^2)$. At most $p_{T_m}-1$ integers are tested.  Each of these integers has $O_{q,k}(\log m)$ bits. By \Cref{lem_primetest}, each test uses $(\log m)^{O(1)}$ bit operations. Consequently, generating the first $T_m$ primes costs $O_{q,k}\left( m^2(\log m)^{O(1)} \right)$ bit operations.
    \item \textbf{Step 5}: Determine the prime $a(\bmb)$. For $1\le j\le T_m$, compute $\Delta_{\bmc}\bmod p_j$ for every $\bmc\in\cB_k(\bmb)\setminus\{\bmb\}$. The first prime for which all these remainders are nonzero is $a(\bmb)$. The analysis preceding this lemma guarantees that such a prime occurs among $p_1,\ldots,p_{T_m}$. There are at most $T_m(D_m-1)=O_{q,k}(m^4\log m)$ remainder computations. For every $\bmc$ and every $j\in[T_m]$, both $\Delta_{\bmc}$ and $p_j$ have $O_{q,k}(\log m)$ bits. Each remainder can be computed in $O_{q,k}((\log m)^2)$ bit operations. Thus, it takes $O_{q,k}(m^4(\log m)^3)$ bit operations to determine the prime $a(\bmb)$ in this step.
    \item \textbf{Step 6}: Finally, compute $r(\bmb)=g_m(\bmb)\bmod a(\bmb)$ and output $\mathsf{H}_m(\bmb)=(a(\bmb),r(\bmb))$. This last remainder computation takes $O_{q,k}((\log m)^2)$ bit operations.
\end{itemize}
Therefore, the computation of $\mathsf{H}_m(\bmb)$ costs
\begin{align*}
    &O_{q,k}(m^3\log m)+O_{q,k}\left( m^8(\log m)^{O(1)} \right)+O_{q,k}(m^2\log m)\\
    &+O_{q,k}\left( m^2(\log m)^{O(1)} \right)+O_{q,k}(m^4(\log m)^3)+O_{q,k}((\log m)^2)\\
    =&O_{q,k}\left( m^8(\log m)^{O(1)} \right)
\end{align*}
bit operations.

\vspace{5pt}
(ii) We next describe the recovery procedure.
Let $(a,r)=\mathsf{H}_m(\bmb)$ and define $\cL(\bmw)
=\cE_{1,k}(\bmw)\cap\Sigma_q^m$. By an argument similar to the proof of \Cref{lem_confusionball}, we can show that $ \left|\cL(\bmw)\right|=O_{q,k}(m)$, and that $\cL(\bmw)$ can be generated without repetitions in $O_{q,k}(m^2\log m)$ bit operations.

For each $\bmc\in\cL(\bmw)$, compute $g_m(\bmc)$
and test whether $g_m(\bmc)\bmod a=r$. The sequence $\bmb$ belongs to $\cL(\bmw)$ and passes this test. Moreover, every $\bmc\in\cL(\bmw)$ belongs to
$\cB_k(\bmb)$. If a sequence $\bmc\ne\bmb$ also passed the test, then $a$ would divide $g_m(\bmb)-g_m(\bmc)$, contrary to the definition of $a=a(\bmb)$. Therefore, exactly one sequence passes the test, and this sequence is $\bmb$.

By \Cref{lem_gm}, one evaluation of $g_m$ costs $O_{q,k}\left( m^6(\log m)^{O(1)} \right)$ bit operations.
There are $O_{q,k}(m)$ candidates. Evaluating $g_m$ on all of them therefore costs
$O_{q,k}\left( m^7(\log m)^{O(1)} \right)$ bit operations.
Since $g_m(\bmc)<P_m^2$ and $a\le p_{T_m}$,
each remainder computation and comparison with $r$
takes $O_{q,k}((\log m)^2)$ bit operations.
All these tests together take
$O_{q,k}(m(\log m)^2)$ bit operations.
Therefore, the recovery procedure costs 
$$O_{q,k}(m^2\log m)+O_{q,k}\left( m^7(\log m)^{O(1)} \right)+O_{q,k}(m(\log m)^2)=O_{q,k}\left( m^7(\log m)^{O(1)} \right)$$
bit operations.
<!-- end ieeeproof-21 -->
<!-- lemma-19: body_md -->
\label{lem_windowcontained}
Let $[i_1,i_2]\subseteq[n]$ with $i_2-i_1<W$. There is $\sigma\in\{0,s\}$ such that the shifted interval $[\sigma+i_1,\sigma+i_2]$ lies in one length-$m$ block of $\bmx^{(\sigma)}$. In other words, there is some $h\in[b_\sigma]$ such that $\left[ \sigma+i_1,\sigma+i_2]\subseteq[(h-1)m+1,hm \right]$.
<!-- end lemma-19 -->
<!-- ieeeproof-22: body_md -->
For each $\sigma\in\{0,s\}$, compute
$\lfloor(i_1+\sigma-1)/m\rfloor$ and
$\lfloor(i_2+\sigma-1)/m\rfloor$. Choose a value of $\sigma$ for which these two integers are equal (such a $\sigma$ must exist), and let $h$ be their common value plus one. Then the index $h$ satisfies our requirement.
<!-- end ieeeproof-22 -->
<!-- lemma-20: body_md -->
\label{lem_completion}
Let $\bmx\in\Sigma_q^n$ and $\bmy$ be obtained from $\bmx$ by a $k$-substring edit. Suppose that an interval
$I=[i_1,i_2]\subseteq[n]$ of length at most $W$ is known and that $I$ covers the edit region in $\bmx$. Then $\bmx$ is uniquely determined by $\bmy$ and
\begin{equation*}
 \left( f_{0,m}(\bmx),g_{0,m}(\bmx), f_{s,m}(\bmx),g_{s,m}(\bmx) \right).
\end{equation*}
The recovery takes
$O_{q,k}(nm^7(\log m)^{O(1)})$ bit operations. In particular, if
$W=(\log n)^{O_{q,k}(1)}$, this is at most 
$n(\log n)^{O_{q,k}(1)}$ for all sufficiently large $n$.
<!-- end lemma-20 -->
<!-- ieeeproof-23: body_md -->
Since the interval $I$ is known, 
we can choose $\sigma\in\{0,s\}$ and $h\in[b_\sigma]$ as in \Cref{lem_windowcontained}. Let $\bmy^{(\sigma)}=0^\sigma\bmy0^{r_{\sigma}}$. The sequence $\bmy^{(\sigma)}$ is obtained from $\bmx^{(\sigma)}$ by a $k$-substring edit. Moreover, the edit region in $\bmx^{(\sigma)}$ is covered by the block $\bmx_h^{(\sigma)}$. Put $d= \left|\bmy\right|- \left|\bmx\right|= \left|\bmy\right|-n$.

For $j<h$, the $j$-th block lies before the edit and therefore, $\bmx_j^{(\sigma)}=\bmy^{(\sigma)}_{[(j-1)m+1,jm]}$. For $j>h$, the $j$-th block lies after the edit and is shifted by $d$, implying $\bmx_j^{(\sigma)}=\bmy^{(\sigma)}_{[(j-1)m+1+d,jm+d]}$. Thus the receiver obtains every block except $\bmx_h^{(\sigma)}$ from $\bmy^{(\sigma)}$ and can compute $a$ and $r$ for all those blocks. It then follows from \eqref{eq_fandg} that
\begin{equation}\label{eq_amrm}
 \begin{aligned}
 a\left( \bmx_h^{(\sigma)} \right)
 &\equiv f_{\sigma,m}(\bmx)
 -\sum_{j\ne h}a\left( \bmx_j^{(\sigma)} \right)
 \pmod{A_m},\\
 r\left( \bmx_h^{(\sigma)} \right)
 &\equiv g_{\sigma,m}(\bmx)
 -\sum_{j\ne h}r\left( \bmx_j^{(\sigma)} \right)
 \pmod{A_m}.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Since $a\left( \bmx_h^{(\sigma)} \right),r\left( \bmx_h^{(\sigma)} \right)\in[0,A_m-1]$, the exact values of $a\left( \bmx_h^{(\sigma)} \right)$ and $r\left( \bmx_h^{(\sigma)} \right)$ are uniquely determined by \eqref{eq_amrm}.

Let $\ell=(h-1)m+1$. Since the edit region in $\bmx^{(\sigma)}$ is covered by $[\ell,\ell+m-1]$, the sequence $\bmy^{(\sigma)}_{[\ell,\ell+m-1+d]}$ is obtained from $\bmx_h^{(\sigma)}$ by the same $k$-substring edit. By \Cref{lem_localrecovery}, sequence $\bmy^{(\sigma)}_{[\ell,\ell+m-1+d]}$ and the recovered pair $\left( a\left( \bmx_h^{(\sigma)} \right),r\left( \bmx_h^{(\sigma)} \right) \right)$ determine
$\bmx_h^{(\sigma)}$ uniquely. Inserting that block among the already known blocks and deleting the $\sigma$ left zeros and the $r_{\sigma}$ right zeros recovers $\bmx$.

We now analyze the bit complexity of the recovery procedure. By the proof of \Cref{lem_windowcontained}, determining $\sigma$ and $h$ requires only a constant number of additions and integer divisions on $O(\log(n+m))$-bit integers, and hence takes $O((\log(n+m))^2)$ bit operations.

We need to compute the syndrome $\mathsf{H}_m$ for the $b_\sigma-1$ known blocks. Since $1\le r_\sigma\le m$ and
$\sigma\in\{0,m/2\}$, we have $b_\sigma-1=\left\lfloor (n+\sigma)/m \right\rfloor\le \frac{2n}{m}$.
By \Cref{lem_localrecovery}, computing the syndrome
$\mathsf{H}_m\left( \bmx_j^{(\sigma)} \right)=\left( a\left( \bmx_j^{(\sigma)} \right),r\left( \bmx_j^{(\sigma)} \right) \right)$
for one known block takes $O_{q,k}\left( m^8(\log m)^{O(1)} \right)$ bit operations. There are at most $2n/m$ such blocks. Therefore, computing all these syndromes takes $O_{q,k}\left( nm^7(\log m)^{O(1)} \right)$ bit operations.

To compute the two sums in \eqref{eq_amrm}, we reduce
each partial sum modulo $A_m$ after every addition.
Thus, every partial sum remains in $[0,A_m-1]$.
Since $A_m=O_{q,k}(m^2(\log m)^2)$, each addition
involves $O_{q,k}(\log m)$-bit integers. Reduction requires at most one subtraction of $A_m$. Consequently, computing both sums and performing the two final modular subtractions in \eqref{eq_amrm} takes $O_{q,k}(b_\sigma\log m)$ bit operations.

With the recovered syndrome $\left( a\left( \bmx_h^{(\sigma)} \right),r\left( \bmx_h^{(\sigma)} \right) \right)$, \Cref{lem_localrecovery}
recovers the missing block $\bmx_h^{(\sigma)}$ in $O_{q,k}\left( m^7(\log m)^{O(1)} \right)$ bit operations. The padded sequences have length $O(n+m)$. Reading the known blocks, extracting the received sequence corresponding to the missing block, and assembling the recovered sequence can therefore be performed by sequential copying in $O_{q,k}(n+m)$ bit operations. If the parameters $P_m$, $T_m$, and $A_m$ have not already been computed, their computation takes $O_{q,k}\left( m^3(\log m)^{O(1)} \right)$ bit operations, as established previously. Combining these costs gives the total recovery complexity $O_{q,k}\left( nm^7(\log m)^{O(1)} \right)$.

Finally, if $W=(\log n)^{O_{q,k}(1)}$, then
$m=2W=(\log n)^{O_{q,k}(1)}$. The factor $m^7(\log m)^{O(1)}$ is consequently bounded by $(\log n)^{O_{q,k}(1)}$ for all sufficiently large $n$. This gives the stated bound $n(\log n)^{O_{q,k}(1)}$.
<!-- end ieeeproof-23 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{thm_codefine}
Fix $q\ge2$ and $k\ge1$. For all sufficiently large $n$, every nonempty set $\cC_{\rm edit}(n;\theta)$ is a $k$-substring edit correcting code. The decoding uses at most $n(\log n)^{O_{q,k}(1)}$ bit operations. Moreover, there is some $\theta$ such that
$$
 \mathsf{red}_2(\cC_{\rm edit}(n;\theta))
 \le \log n+8\log\log n+8\log\log\log n+O_{q,k}(1).
$$
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- ieeeproof-24: body_md -->
Let $\bmx\in\cC_{\rm edit}(n;\theta)$, and let $\bmy$ be obtained from $\bmx$ by one $k$-substring edit. The syndrome $\mathsf{Loc}_{\rm edit}(\bmx)$ is part of the fixed value $\theta$. By \Cref{thm_specificlocator}, the receiver first determines whether $\bmy=\bmx$.  If equality holds, it outputs $\bmy$. Otherwise, \Cref{thm_specificlocator} gives an interval of length at most $W_{n,k}$ containing the edit region in $\bmx$.
The remaining four entries of $\theta$ are exactly the four values required in \Cref{lem_completion}, which then recovers $\bmx$ uniquely. Therefore, the code $\cC_{\rm edit}(n;\theta)$ is a $k$-substring edit correcting code.

Next, we estimate the decoding complexity. The receiver uses \Cref{thm_specificlocator} to test for no error and, otherwise, to locate the edit. This costs $O_{q,k}(n(\log n)^2)$ bit operations. For a nontrivial edit, \Cref{lem_completion} recovers the sequence using $O_{q,k}\left( nm^7(\log m)^{O(1)} \right)$ additional bit operations. That bound includes computing the known block syndromes, recovering the missing syndrome by modular subtraction, and recovering and inserting the missing block. For no error, copying the received sequence costs $O_q(n)$ bit operations. Hence the same bound covers both cases. Substituting $m=\Theta_{q,k}(\log n)$ gives the bound $n(\log n)^{O_{q,k}(1)}$.

To bound the redundancy, we need to bound the number of possible values of $\theta$. By \eqref{eq_LocSyn}, the first component $\mathsf{Loc}_{\rm edit}(\bmx)$ has at most $5M_{n,k}R_{k}\left( 2\Lambda_{q,k}+1 \right)^2$ values. Each of the remaining four components is a residue modulo $A_{m}$. Hence, the number of possible values of $\theta$ is at most $5M_{n,k}R_{k}\left( 2\Lambda_{q,k}+1 \right)^2A_m^4$.
By \Cref{lem_pdense}, $ \left|\cD_{q,k}(n)\right|\ge q^{n-1}$. Therefore, the pigeonhole principle ensures that there is a choice of $\theta$ such that
$$
  \left|\cC_{\rm edit}(n;\theta)\right|\ge\frac{q^{n-1}}{5M_{n,k}R_{k}\left( 2\Lambda_{q,k}+1 \right)^2A_m^4},
$$
or equivalently,
$$
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathsf{red}_2\left( \cC_{\rm edit}(n;\theta) \right)
 &\le \log q+\log\left( 5M_{n,k}R_{k}\left( 2\Lambda_{q,k}+1 \right)^2 \right)
 +4\log A_{m}\\
 &\le \log n+8\log m
 +8\log\log m+O_{q,k}(1).
 \end{aligned}
$$
Indeed, $M_{n,k}=\Theta_{q,k}(n)$, whereas $R_k$ and
$2\Lambda_{q,k}+1$ are constants for fixed $q,k$, and
$A_m=O_{q,k}(m^2(\log m)^2)$. By \eqref{eq_ellnk}, we have $\ell_{n,k}=\Theta_{q,k}(\log n)$. It follows from \eqref{eq_Amu} and \eqref{eq_specificinterval} that $A_{n,k}=\Theta_{q,k}(\log n)$ and $W_{n,k}=\Theta_{q,k}(\log n)$. Hence
$m=\Theta_{q,k}(\log n)$, and therefore
$$
 \log m=\log\log n+O_{q,k}(1),
 \;
 \log\log m=\log\log\log n+O_{q,k}(1),
$$
which proves the redundancy bound.
<!-- end ieeeproof-24 -->
<!-- definition-7: body_md -->
\label{def_unequalball}
For $\bmx\in\Sigma_q^n$, define
\begin{equation}\label{uneq_eq_model}
 \cE_{1,k}^{\ne}(\bmx)\triangleq\left\{\bmz\in\cE_{1,k}(\bmx): \left|\bmz\right|\ne n\right\}.
\end{equation}
A nonempty set $\cC\subseteq\Sigma_q^n$ is an \emph{unequal-length $k$-substring edit correcting code} if $\cE_{1,k}^{\ne}(\bmx)\cap\cE_{1,k}^{\ne}(\bmy)=\varnothing$ for any distinct $\bmx,\bmy\in\cC$.
<!-- end definition-7 -->
<!-- definition-8: body_md -->
\label{def_marker}
Let $\bmx\in\Sigma_q^n$. A coordinate $i\in\mathbb Z$ is called \emph{special} (with respect to $\bmx$) if $x_i\ne x_{i+\ell_{\ast}}$. A special coordinate $i$ is called a \emph{marker} of $\bmx$ if $\bmx_{[i,i+\ell_{\ast}]}<\bmx_{[j,j+\ell_{\ast}]}$ for every special coordinate $j$ satisfying $0< \left|j-i\right|\le k$. In particular, a special coordinate is a marker if there is no other special coordinate within distance $k$ from it.
<!-- end definition-8 -->
<!-- definition-9: body_md -->
Let $\bmx\in\Sigma_q^n$. We associate with it a binary sequence $\bm{\chi}_k(\bmx)=\chi_1\chi_2\cdots\chi_n$, which is defined as follows:
$$
\chi_i=
\begin{cases}
    1,\mbox{ if }i\text{ is a marker},\\
    0,\mbox{ else}.
\end{cases}
$$
We call $\bm{\chi}_k(\bmx)$ the \emph{marker sequence} of $\bmx$.
<!-- end definition-9 -->
<!-- example-3: body_md -->
Let $q=2$, $k=1$. Then $\ell_{\ast}=1$.
\begin{itemize}
    \item For $\bmx=001100$, the special coordinates are $2$ and $4$, since $x_2\ne x_3$ and $x_4\ne x_5$. The distance between the two special coordinates is $2>k$, and so each is a marker. Hence $\bm{\chi}_1(\bmx)=010100$.
    \item For $\bmx=100$, the special coordinates are $0$ and $1$. Since $01<10$, coordinate $0$ is a marker and coordinate $1$ is not. Only markers in $[3]$ are considered in the definition of the marker sequence, so $\bm{\chi}_1(\bmx)=000$. This example shows that a coordinate outside $[n]$ can affect whether a coordinate inside $[n]$ is a marker.
\end{itemize}
<!-- end example-3 -->
<!-- example-4: body_md -->
\label{uneq_ex_markers}
Let $q=3$, $k=2$, and $\bmx=012012221$. Then $\ell_{\ast}=2$. The special coordinates and their corresponding sequences are given in the following table.
$$
\begin{array}{c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c}
\hline
 i&0&1&2&3&4&5&7&8&9\\
 \hline
 \bmx_{[i,i+2]}&001&012&120&201&012&122&221&210&100\\
 \hline
\end{array}
$$
At each of $0,4,9$, the sequence $\bmx_{[i,i+2]}$ is smaller than the sequence at every other special coordinate within distance two. Each of the remaining special coordinates has a smaller such neighbor. Thus the markers are $0,4,9$, and $\bm{\chi}_2(\bmx)=000100001$. Notice that $\bmx_{[1,3]}=\bmx_{[4,6]}$, but the distance between these two special coordinates is greater than $k$.
<!-- end example-4 -->
<!-- definition-10: body_md -->
A binary sequence is called \emph{$k$-sparse} if there are at least $k$ symbols $0$ between any two symbols $1$.
<!-- end definition-10 -->
<!-- lemma-21: body_md -->
\label{lem_markerproperty}
For every $\bmx\in\Sigma_q^n$, the following statements hold.
\begin{enumerate}[$(i)$]
\item If $i$ is special, then $\bmx_{[i,i+\ell_{\ast}]}\ne\bmx_{[j,j+\ell_{\ast}]}$ for any $j\in\mathbb{Z}$ with $0< \left|i-j\right|\le k$. Moreover, $\bm{\chi}_k(\bmx)$ is $k$-sparse.
\item For every special coordinate $i$, there is a marker $j\in\mathbb Z$ such that $ \left|i-j\right|\le k(D_{\ast}-1)$.
\item Whether $i$ is a marker is determined by $x_{i-k},\ldots,x_{i+\ell_{\ast}+k}$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-21 -->
<!-- ieeeproof-25: body_md -->
For (i), suppose
$\bmx_{[i,i+\ell_{\ast}]}=\bmx_{[i+a,i+a+\ell_{\ast}]}$ for some integer $a$ with $ \left|a\right|\in[k]$, where $i$ is special. Then $x_{i+r}=x_{i+a+r}$ for every $r\in[0,\ell_{\ast}]$. By \eqref{eq_uneqpara1}, we have $a\mid\ell_{\ast}$. If $a>0$, then taking $r=0,a,\ldots,\ell_{\ast}-a$ gives $x_i=x_{i+a}=\cdots=x_{i+\ell_{\ast}}$. If $a<0$, then taking $r=\ell_{\ast},\ell_{\ast}+a,\ldots,0$ gives $x_{i+\ell_{\ast}}=x_{i+\ell_{\ast}+a}=\cdots=x_i$. Both cases contradict the assumption that $i$ is special. Thus the length-$(\ell_{\ast}+1)$ sequence corresponding to a special coordinate must be different from any length-$(\ell_{\ast}+1)$ sequence within distance at most $k$ from it.

If two markers were at distance at most $k$, then,
by \Cref{def_marker}, each of their corresponding
sequences would have to be strictly smaller than the
other, which is impossible. Therefore, any two markers
have distance at least $k+1$, implying that
$\bm{\chi}_k(\bmx)$ is $k$-sparse.

For (ii), the number of length-$(\ell_{\ast}+1)$ sequences with different first and last symbols is $q\cdot q^{\ell_{\ast}-1}(q-1)=D_{\ast}$. If a special coordinate is not a marker, then, by (i) and \Cref{def_marker}, there is another special coordinate within distance $k$ whose corresponding sequence is strictly smaller. Starting from $i$, repeatedly choose such a coordinate. The corresponding sequences strictly decrease and belong to a set of size $D_{\ast}$. Hence this procedure stops at a marker after at most $D_{\ast}-1$ choices. The total distance from $i$ is at most $k(D_{\ast}-1)$. This proves (ii).

For (iii), all sequences compared with $\bmx_{[i,i+\ell_{\ast}]}$ start in $[i-k,i+k]$ and end in $[i-k+\ell_{\ast},i+k+\ell_{\ast}]$. Thus the symbols in $[i-k,i+\ell_{\ast}+k]$ determine the special coordinates being compared and all the comparisons.
<!-- end ieeeproof-25 -->
<!-- example-5: body_md -->
For the sequence in \Cref{uneq_ex_markers}, the markers in $[9]$ are $4$ and $9$, so the segments are $\bmu_0^x=0012$, $\bmu_1^x=01222$, and $\bmu_2^x=1$. Since $B_{\ast}=37$, the label of $\bmu_0^x$ is $\mathsf{Lab}\left( \bmu_0^x \right)=0012\,0^{33}0012\,0^{33}$.
<!-- end example-5 -->
<!-- lemma-22: body_md -->
\label{lem_label}
Every segment $\bmu_j^x$ is uniquely determined by its length $ \left|\bmu_j^x\right|$ and its label $\mathsf{Lab}\left( \bmu_j^x \right)$.
<!-- end lemma-22 -->
<!-- ieeeproof-26: body_md -->
Let $m= \left|\bmu_j^x\right|$ and $d=\min\left\{m,B_{\ast}\right\}$. If $m\le 2B_{\ast}$, then $m-d\le d$. Now the conclusion is clear from the definition of $\mathsf{Lab}\left( \bmu_j^x \right)$. Next, assume $m>2B_{\ast}$. Then $d=B_{\ast}$.

Recall that $\bmu_j^{x}=x_{s_j}\cdots x_{s_{j+1}-1}$. We can obtain $\bmx_{\left[ s_j,s_j+B_{\ast}-1 \right]}$ and $\bmx_{\left[ s_{j+1}-B_{\ast},s_{j+1}-1 \right]}$ immediately from $\mathsf{Lab}\left( \bmu_j^x \right)$. It remains to determine the unknown part $\bmx_{\left[ s_j+B_{\ast},s_{j+1}-B_{\ast}-1 \right]}$. We claim that
\begin{equation}\label{eq_periodell}
    x_{i}=x_{i-\ell_{\ast}}
\end{equation}
for every $i\in\left[ s_j+B_{\ast},s_{j+1}-B_{\ast}-1 \right]$. Suppose on the contrary that there is an $i\in\left[ s_j+B_{\ast},s_{j+1}-B_{\ast}-1 \right]$ such that $x_{i}\ne x_{i-\ell_{\ast}}$. Then the index $i-\ell_{\ast}$ is special with respect to $\bmx$. According to \Cref{lem_markerproperty}~(ii), there is a marker in the interval $\left[ i-\ell_{\ast}-k(D_{\ast}-1),i-\ell_{\ast}+k(D_{\ast}-1) \right]$. Since $i\in\left[ s_j+B_{\ast},s_{j+1}-B_{\ast}-1 \right]$ and $B_{\ast}=k(D_{\ast}-1)+\ell_{\ast}+1$, we have $\left[ i-\ell_{\ast}-k(D_{\ast}-1),i-\ell_{\ast}+k(D_{\ast}-1) \right]\subseteq\left[ s_j+1,s_{j+1}-1 \right]$. However, by the definition of $s_0,s_1,\ldots,s_{w+1}$, there is no marker in $[s_{j}+1,s_{j+1}-1]$ for any $j\in[0,w]$. This proves \eqref{eq_periodell}.

Now the substring $\bmx_{\left[ s_j+B_{\ast},s_{j+1}-B_{\ast}-1 \right]}$ can be recovered from $\bmx_{\left[ s_j,s_j+B_{\ast}-1 \right]}$ by \eqref{eq_periodell}, since $B_{\ast}\ge\ell_{\ast}$. This completes the proof.
<!-- end ieeeproof-26 -->
<!-- definition-11: body_md -->
\label{def_tsburst}
    Let $r_1,r_2\ge0$ be integers with $r_1+r_2>0$. Let $\bmalpha\in\Sigma^n$ where $n\ge r_1$, and $p\in[1,n-r_1+1]$. A sequence $\bmbeta\in\Sigma^{n-r_1+r_2}$ is obtained from $\bmalpha$ by an \emph{$(r_1,r_2)$-burst error} at position $p$, if $\beta_j=\alpha_j$ for every $j<p$ and $\beta_j=\alpha_{j+r_1-r_2}$ for every $j\ge p+r_2$. Thus the error replaces $r_1$ consecutive symbols by a sequence of length $r_2$ at the same position.
<!-- end definition-11 -->
<!-- lemma-23: body_md -->
\label{lem_inducedThburst}
Let $n\ge T_{\ast}$, $\bmx\in\Sigma_q^n$ and $\bmx\to_k\bmy$. Represent $\bmx$ and $\bmy$ as in \eqref{eq_editbasic} with the convention in \eqref{eq_minimalends}. Denote $i= \left|\bmu\right|+1$, $s= \left|\bms\right|$ and $t= \left|\bmt\right|$. Let $h=s-t$. Then $\bm{\chi}_k(\bmy)$ is obtained from $\bm{\chi}_k(\bmx)$ by a $(T_{\ast},T_{\ast}-h)$-burst error at some position $p\in[1,n-T_{\ast}+1]$. The position $p$ can be chosen so that $i\in[p,p+T_{\ast}-s]$ or equivalently, the interval $\left[ p,p+T_{\ast}-1 \right]$ covers the edit region in $\bmx$.
<!-- end lemma-23 -->
<!-- ieeeproof-27: body_md -->
By assumption, it holds that $y_j=x_j$ when $j<i$ and $y_j=x_{j+h}$ when $j\ge i+t$. Then by \Cref{lem_markerproperty}~(iii), we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq_chixy}
\chi_k(\bmy)_j=
\begin{cases}
   \bm{\chi}_k(\bmx)_j,\mbox{ if }j< i-\ell_{\ast}-k,\\
   \bm{\chi}_k(\bmx)_{j+h},\mbox{ if }j\ge i+t+k.
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
Let $u=\max\{1,i-\ell_{\ast}-k\}$ and $v=\min\{n,i+s+k-1\}$. Then $[u,v]=\left[ i-\ell_{\ast}-k,i+s+k-1 \right]\cap[n]$ and $v-u+1\le\ell_{\ast}+2k+s\le T_{\ast}$. \Cref{eq_chixy} implies that all errors in $\bm{\chi}_k(\bmx)$ occurred in the interval $[u,v]$. Notice that $ \left|\bm{\chi}_k(\bmy)\right|= \left|\bm{\chi}_k(\bmx)\right|-h$. Then by \Cref{def_tsburst}, to prove this lemma, it suffices to find an index $p\in[1,n-T_{\ast}+1]$, such that $[u,v]\subseteq\left[ p,p+T_{\ast}-1 \right]$ and $i\in[p,p+T_{\ast}-s]$.

Set $p=\min\{u,n-T_{\ast}+1\}$. If $p=u$, then $v\le u+T_{\ast}-1=p+T_{\ast}-1$. If $p=n-T_{\ast}+1$, then $v\le n=p+T_{\ast}-1$. In either case, we have $[u,v]\subseteq[p,p+T_{\ast}-1]$.

Now it remains to show $i\in[p,p+T_{\ast}-s]$. First, notice that $u=\max\left\{1,i-\ell_{\ast}-k\right\}$, $p\le u$ and $i,\ell_{\ast},k\ge1$. This gives $p\le i$. By the definition of a $k$-substring edit, we have $1\le i+s\le n+1$. If $i+s\le n$, then $i+s\le v<p+T_{\ast}$, since $k\ge1$. If $i+s=n+1$, then $v=n$ and $p+T_{\ast}=n+1\ge i+s$. Now the proof is completed.
<!-- end ieeeproof-27 -->
<!-- lemma-24: body_md -->
\label{lem_markervt}
Suppose that $\bmbeta$ is obtained from $\bmalpha\in\Sigma_2^n$ by a $(T_{\ast},T_{\ast}-h)$-burst-error at position $p$, where $1\le \left|h\right|\le k$. Put $\Delta_{L_1}=\mathsf{wt}_{L_1}\left(\bmalpha\right)-\mathsf{wt}_{L_1}\left(\bmbeta\right)$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq_markervt}
 \mathsf{VT}(\bmalpha)-\mathsf{VT}(\bmbeta)
 =p\Delta_{L_1}+h\sum_{i=p+1}^{n-h}\beta_i+\delta_p,
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation*}
    \delta_p=
 \sum_{i=1}^{T_{\ast}-1}i\alpha_{p+i}
 -\sum_{i=1}^{T_{\ast}-h-1}(i+h)\beta_{p+i}.
\end{equation*}
Clearly, it holds that $ \left|\delta_p\right|\le E_{\ast}/2$.
<!-- end lemma-24 -->
<!-- ieeeproof-28: body_md -->
By assumption, we have $\beta_i=\alpha_i$ for every $i<p$, and $\beta_i=\alpha_{i+h}$ for every $i\ge p+T_{\ast}-h$. Then it follows from the definition of the function $\mathsf{VT}$ that
\begin{equation*}
\begin{aligned}
\mathsf{VT}(\bmalpha)-\mathsf{VT}(\bmbeta)
 &=\sum_{i=p}^{p+T_{\ast}-1}i\alpha_i+\sum_{i=p+T_{\ast}}^{n}i\alpha_i-\sum_{i=p}^{p+T_{\ast}-h-1}i\beta_i-\sum_{i=p+T_{\ast}-h}^{n-h}i\beta_i\\
 &=p\left( \sum_{i=p}^{p+T_{\ast}-1}\alpha_i-\sum_{i=p}^{p+T_{\ast}-h-1}\beta_i \right)+\sum_{i=1}^{T_{\ast}-1}i\alpha_{p+i}-\sum_{i=1}^{T_{\ast}-h-1}i\beta_{p+i}+h\sum_{i=p+T_{\ast}-h}^{n-h}\beta_i\\
 &=p\Delta_{L_1}+\sum_{i=1}^{T_{\ast}-1}i\alpha_{p+i}-\sum_{i=1}^{T_{\ast}-h-1}(i+h)\beta_{p+i}+h\sum_{i=p+1}^{n-h}\beta_i\\
 &=p\Delta_{L_1}+h\sum_{i=p+1}^{n-h}\beta_i+\delta_p.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation*}

Finally, since
\begin{gather*}
    \left|\sum_{i=1}^{T_{\ast}-1}i\alpha_{p+i}\right|\le\sum_{i=1}^{T_{\ast}-1}i=\binom{T_{\ast}}{2},\\
    \left|\sum_{i=1}^{T_{\ast}-h-1}(i+h)\beta_{p+i}\right|\le\sum_{i=1}^{T_{\ast}+k-1}(i+k)=\frac{(T_{\ast}+3k)(T_{\ast}+k-1)}{2},
\end{gather*}
we conclude that $ \left|\delta_p\right|\le U_{\ast}(U_{\ast}-1)+\binom{k}{2}=E_{\ast}/2$.
<!-- end ieeeproof-28 -->
<!-- lemma-25: body_md -->
(\textit{cf.}~\cite[Lemma 4]{Yubo2025tsburstIT})\label{lem_indexdistance}
Let $\bma,\bmb\in\mathbb{Z}_{\ge0}^n$, and let $K,Q$ be positive integers. Suppose that $ \left|a_i-b_i\right|<Q$ for every $i\in[n]$, and
$\sum_{i\in[n]:\,i\equiv r\pmod K}a_i\equiv\sum_{i\in[n]:\,i\equiv r\pmod K}b_i\pmod Q$
for every $r\in[0,K-1]$. If $\bma$ and $\bmb$ agree outside an interval containing at most $K$ coordinates, then $\bma=\bmb$.
<!-- end lemma-25 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label{prop_markerrecovery}
Let $n\ge T_{\ast}$ and $1\le \left|h\right|\le k$. Suppose that a $k$-sparse sequence $\bmbeta\in\Sigma_2^{n-h}$ is obtained from $\bmalpha,\bmgamma\in\Sigma_2^n$ by $(T_{\ast},T_{\ast}-h)$-burst errors. If $\mathsf{H}_{n}^{\ne}(\bmalpha)=\mathsf{H}_{n}^{\ne}(\bmgamma)$, then $\bmalpha=\bmgamma$.

As a corollary, if $\bmy\in\cE_{1,k}^{\ne}(\bmx)$ for some $\bmx\in\Sigma_q^n$, then given $\mathsf{H}_{n}^{\ne}\left( \bm{\chi}_k(\bmx) \right)$, one can uniquely recover $\bm{\chi}_k(\bmx)$ from $\bm{\chi}_k(\bmy)$.
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- ieeeproof-29: body_md -->
Suppose that the $(T_{\ast},T_{\ast}-h)$-burst errors in $\bmalpha$ and $\bmgamma$ occurred at positions $p$ and $r$, respectively. Without loss of generality, we may assume $1\le p\le r\le n-T_{\ast}+1$. Let $I=[p,r+T_{\ast}-1]$. Then it holds that
\begin{equation}\label{eq_albega}
    \begin{gathered}
        \bmalpha_{[n]\setminus I}=\bmgamma_{[n]\setminus I},\\
        \bmbeta_{[1,p-1]}=\bmalpha_{[1,p-1]},\\
        \beta_i=\alpha_{i+h},\text{ for }i\ge r+T_{\ast}-h.
    \end{gathered}
\end{equation}

The assumption $\mathsf{wt}_{L_1}\left(\bmalpha\right)\equiv\mathsf{wt}_{L_1}\left(\bmgamma\right)\pmod{2U_{\ast}}$ gives $\mathsf{wt}_{L_1}\left(\bmalpha\right)-\mathsf{wt}_{L_1}\left(\bmbeta\right)\equiv\mathsf{wt}_{L_1}\left(\bmgamma\right)-\mathsf{wt}_{L_1}\left(\bmbeta\right)\pmod{2U_{\ast}}$. Since $\bmbeta$ is obtained from both $\bmalpha$ and $\bmgamma$ by $(T_{\ast},T_{\ast}-h)$-burst-errors, we have $\mathsf{wt}_{L_1}\left(\bmalpha\right)-\mathsf{wt}_{L_1}\left(\bmbeta\right),\mathsf{wt}_{L_1}\left(\bmgamma\right)-\mathsf{wt}_{L_1}\left(\bmbeta\right)\in\left[ -(T_{\ast}-h),T_{\ast} \right]\subseteq\left[ -U_{\ast},U_{\ast}-1 \right]$. Then it follows that $\mathsf{wt}_{L_1}\left(\bmalpha\right)-\mathsf{wt}_{L_1}\left(\bmbeta\right)=\mathsf{wt}_{L_1}\left(\bmgamma\right)-\mathsf{wt}_{L_1}\left(\bmbeta\right)$. Denote $\Delta_{L_1}=\mathsf{wt}_{L_1}\left(\bmalpha\right)-\mathsf{wt}_{L_1}\left(\bmbeta\right)$.

Let $L=r-p$ and $S=\sum_{i=p+1}^{r}\beta_i$. Applying \eqref{eq_markervt} to the two pairs $\left( \bmalpha,\bmbeta \right)$ and $\left( \bmgamma,\bmbeta \right)$ yields
\begin{equation}\label{eq_alphagamma}
    \mathsf{VT}(\bmgamma)-\mathsf{VT}(\bmalpha)=L\Delta_{L_1}-hS+\delta_r-\delta_p.
\end{equation}
Since $S\le L\le n-T_{\ast}<n$, $ \left|\Delta_{L_1}\right|\le U_{\ast}$ and $ \left|h\right|\le k<U_{\ast}$, we have
\begin{align*}
     \left|\mathsf{VT}(\bmgamma)-\mathsf{VT}(\bmalpha)\right|&\le L \left|\Delta_{L_1}\right|+ \left|h\right|S+ \left|\delta_r\right|+ \left|\delta_p\right|\\
    &<(U_{\ast}+k)n+E_{\ast}=M_{n,\ast}.
\end{align*}
Then the condition $\mathsf{VT}(\bmalpha)\equiv \mathsf{VT}(\bmgamma)\pmod{M_{n,\ast}}$ implies $\mathsf{VT}(\bmalpha)=\mathsf{VT}(\bmgamma)$. Combining this with \eqref{eq_alphagamma} gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq_LDelta}
 L\Delta_{L_1}=hS+\delta_p-\delta_r.
\end{equation}
Since $\bmbeta$ is $k$-sparse, an interval of length $L\ge1$ contains at most $S\le(L+k)/(k+1)$ ones. For $L=0$, we have $S=0$. Thus the bound $S\le(L+k)/(k+1)$ always holds.

Suppose first that $\Delta_{L_1}\ne0$. It follows from \eqref{eq_LDelta} that $L\le L \left|\Delta_{L_1}\right|\le kS+E_{\ast}\le k(L+k)/(k+1)+E_{\ast}$.
Hence $L\le k^2+(k+1)E_{\ast}$ and
$ \left|I\right|=L+T_{\ast}\le k^2+(k+1)E_{\ast}+U_{\ast}-k=K_{\ast}$.
Recall from \eqref{eq_albega} that $\alpha_i\ne\gamma_i$ only if $i\in I$. Therefore, the condition $P_r(\bmalpha)=P_r(\bmgamma)$ for every $r\in[0,K_{\ast}-1]$ gives $\bmalpha=\bmgamma$ by \Cref{lem_indexdistance}, with $K=K_{\ast}$ and $Q=2$.

Now suppose $\Delta_{L_1}=0$. Then $\mathsf{wt}_{L_1}\left(\bmalpha\right)=\mathsf{wt}_{L_1}\left(\bmbeta\right)=\mathsf{wt}_{L_1}\left(\bmgamma\right)$.  Denote $w=\mathsf{wt}_{L_1}\left(\bmalpha\right)$. If $w=0$, then $\bmalpha=\bmgamma=0^n$. Otherwise, let $L_j^{\alpha},L_j^{\beta},L_j^{\gamma}$ be the positions of the $j$-th one in $\bmalpha,\bmbeta,\bmgamma$, respectively. Let $w_1=\mathsf{wt}_{L_1}\left(\bmalpha_{[1,p-1]}\right)$ and $w_2=\mathsf{wt}_{L_1}\left(\bmalpha_{I}\right)$. It follows from \eqref{eq_albega} that $\mathsf{wt}_{L_1}\left(\bmbeta_{[1,p-1]}\right)=\mathsf{wt}_{L_1}\left(\bmgamma_{[1,p-1]}\right)=w_1$, $\mathsf{wt}_{L_1}\left(\bmbeta_{[p,r+T_{\ast}-h-1]}\right)=\mathsf{wt}_{L_1}\left(\bmgamma_{I}\right)=w_2$ and $\mathsf{wt}_{L_1}\left(\bmbeta_{[r+T_{\ast}-h,n-h]}\right)=\mathsf{wt}_{L_1}\left(\bmalpha_{[r+T_{\ast},n]}\right)=\mathsf{wt}_{L_1}\left(\bmgamma_{[r+T_{\ast},n]}\right)=w-w_1-w_2$. Consequently,
\begin{equation*}
\begin{gathered}
     L_j^{\beta}=L_j^{\alpha}=L_j^{\gamma},\text{ for }j\le w_1,\\
     L_j^{\beta}=L_{j}^{\alpha}-h=L_{j}^{\gamma}-h,\text{ for }j>w_1+w_2.
\end{gathered}
\end{equation*}
This gives $ \left|L_j^{\alpha}-L_j^{\gamma}\right|=0$ for $j\in [w_1]\cup[w_1+w_2+1,w]$. For every $j\in[w_1+1,w_1+w_2]$, since $ \left|L_j^{\alpha}-L_j^{\beta}\right|, \left|L_j^{\gamma}-L_j^{\beta}\right|<\max\left\{T_{\ast},T_{\ast}-h\right\}\le U_{\ast}$, it holds that $ \left|L_j^{\alpha}-L_j^{\gamma}\right|<2U_{\ast}$.

If $L_j^{\alpha}= L_j^{\gamma}$ for every $j\in[w]$, the conclusion follows. Otherwise, let $j_1$ and $j_2$ be the smallest and largest indices $j$ such that $L_j^{\alpha}\ne L_j^{\gamma}$, respectively. To apply \Cref{lem_indexdistance}, it remains to bound $j_2-j_1$. First, notice that $j_1,j_2\in[w_1+1,w_1+w_2]$ and thus, $j_2-j_1<w_2$. The assumption $\Delta_{L_1}= 0$ and \eqref{eq_LDelta} give $ \left|h\right|S\le E_{\ast}$. Since $h\ne0$, it follows that $S\le E_{\ast}$. Therefore,
$$
 w_2=\beta_p+\sum_{i=p+1}^{r}\beta_i+
 \sum_{i=r+1}^{r+T_{\ast}-h-1}\beta_i
 \le S+T_{\ast}-h\le E_{\ast}+U_{\ast}<K_{\ast}.
$$
Now the condition $R_r(\bmalpha)=R_r(\bmgamma)$ for every $r\in[0,K_{\ast}-1]$, together with \Cref{lem_indexdistance} with $Q=2U_{\ast}$ and $K=K_{\ast}$, gives $L_j^{\alpha}=L_j^{\gamma}$ for every $j\in[w]$, which implies $\bmalpha=\bmgamma$. Now the proof is completed.
<!-- end ieeeproof-29 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
\begin{itemize}
        \item The hypotheses in \Cref{prop_markerrecovery} require the common received sequence $\bmbeta$ to be $k$-sparse. The two original binary sequences need not be $k$-sparse.
        \item The condition $h\ne0$ is used to deduce $S\le E_{\ast}$ when $\Delta_{L_1}=0$. If $h=\Delta_{L_1}=0$, \eqref{eq_LDelta} gives no bound on $S$.
    \end{itemize}
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- lemma-26: body_md -->
\label{lem_affectedsegments}
Let $\bmx,\bmz\in\Sigma_q^n$ where $n\ge T_{\ast}$. Suppose $\bm{\chi}_k(\bmx)=\bm{\chi}_k(\bmz)$ and $\cE_{1,k}^{\ne}(\bmx)\cap\cE_{1,k}^{\ne}(\bmz)\ne\emptyset$. Let $w=\mathsf{wt}_{L_1}\left(\bm{\chi}_k(\bmx)\right)=\mathsf{wt}_{L_1}\left(\bm{\chi}_k(\bmz)\right)$. Denote the respective segments of $\bmx$ and $\bmz$ by $\bmu_0^x,\ldots,\bmu_w^x$ and $\bmu_0^z,\ldots,\bmu_w^z$. Then there exist integers $0\le a\le b\le w$ such that $b-a+1\le2U_{\ast}$ and $\bmu_j^x=\bmu_j^z$ for every $j\in[0,w]\setminus[a,b]$. In other words, the two sequences $\bmx$ and $\bmz$ differ in at most $2U_{\ast}$ segments.
<!-- end lemma-26 -->
<!-- ieeeproof-30: body_md -->
Let $\bmy\in\cE_{1,k}^{\ne}(\bmx)\cap\cE_{1,k}^{\ne}(\bmz)$ and $h=n- \left|\bmy\right|$. Then $1\le \left|h\right|\le k$.
Suppose that the $k$-substring edits in $\bmx$ and $\bmz$ start at positions $i_x$ and $i_z$ and remove $s_x$ and $s_z$ symbols, respectively, where $s_x,s_z\in[0,k]$.

Denote $\bmalpha=\bm{\chi}_k(\bmx)=\bm{\chi}_k(\bmz)$ and
$\bmbeta=\bm{\chi}_k(\bmy)$. Since $\bmy\in\cE_{1,k}^{\ne}(\bmx)$, by \Cref{lem_inducedThburst}, there exists $p\in[1,n-T_{\ast}+1]$ such that $\bmbeta$ is obtained from $\bmalpha$ by a $(T_{\ast},T_{\ast}-h)$-burst error at position $p$, where the index $p$ satisfies $i_x\in\left[ p,p+T_{\ast}-s_x \right]$. By the same argument, there exists $r\in[1,n-T_{\ast}+1]$ such that
$\bmbeta$ is obtained from $\bmalpha$ by
a $(T_{\ast},T_{\ast}-h)$-burst error at position $r$, and the index $r$ satisfies $i_z\in\left[ r,r+T_{\ast}-s_z \right]$.

Without loss of generality, assume $p\le r$. Then we have
$$
 x_j=z_j=
 \begin{cases}
  y_j,     & j\in[1,p-1],\\
  y_{j-h}, & j\in[r+T_{\ast},n].
 \end{cases}
$$
Consequently, $x_j=z_j$ for every $j\in[n]\setminus I$,
where $I\triangleq[p,r+T_{\ast}-1]$.

We next prove that $\sum_{j\in I}\alpha_j<2U_{\ast}$.
Put $L=r-p$. If $L\le U_{\ast}$, then, since $T_{\ast}=U_{\ast}-k$ and $k\ge1$, $ \left|I\right|=L+T_{\ast}\le2U_{\ast}-k<2U_{\ast}$. The desired bound follows because $\bmalpha$ is binary.

Now assume $L>U_{\ast}$. The $(T_{\ast},T_{\ast}-h)$-burst error at position $r$ gives $\beta_j=\alpha_j$ for $j\in[1,r-1]$, whereas the $(T_{\ast},T_{\ast}-h)$-burst
error at position $p$ gives $\beta_j=\alpha_{j+h}$ for
$j\in[p+T_{\ast}-h,n-h]$. Hence, for every $j\in[p+T_{\ast}-h,r-1]$, it holds that $\alpha_j=\beta_j=\alpha_{j+h}$. All these coordinates are valid: the bounds on $p,r$ and $T_{\ast}> \left|h\right|$ imply that $j\in[n]\cap[n-h]$ and $j+h\in[n]$.
If $\alpha_j=1$, then $\alpha_{j+h}=1$. This contradicts the $k$-sparsity of $\bmalpha$ established in \Cref{lem_markerproperty}~(i), since $1\le \left|(j+h)-j\right|= \left|h\right|\le k$. Therefore, $\alpha_j=0$ throughout
$[p+T_{\ast}-h,r-1]$. Then it follows that
$$
 \begin{aligned}
 \sum_{j\in I}\alpha_j
 &=\sum_{j=p}^{p+T_{\ast}-h-1}\alpha_j
   +\sum_{j=r}^{r+T_{\ast}-1}\alpha_j\\
 &\le (T_{\ast}-h)+T_{\ast}
 <2U_{\ast}.
 \end{aligned}
$$
The three subintervals of $I$ used here are illustrated in
\Cref{uneq_fig_interval}.

Let $s_1<\cdots<s_w$ be the common markers in $[n]$, and put $s_0=0$ and $s_{w+1}=n+1$. Let $a,b\in[0,w]$ be the unique indices satisfying $s_a\le p<s_{a+1}$ and
$s_b\le r+T_{\ast}-1<s_{b+1}$. Then $a\le b$. The set of markers in $[p+1,r+T_{\ast}-1]$ is $\left\{s_{a+1},\ldots,s_b\right\}$. By definition of $\bmalpha$, we have $\alpha_j=1$ if and only if $j\in\left\{s_1,\ldots,s_w\right\}$. Therefore,
$$
 b-a+1
 =1+\sum_{j=p+1}^{r+T_{\ast}-1}\alpha_j
 \le1+\sum_{j\in I}\alpha_j
 \le2U_{\ast}.
$$
It follows from the choice of $a$ and $b$ that $I$ is a subset of $[s_a,s_{b+1}-1]$. Since $x_i=z_i$ for every $i\in[n]\setminus I$, we conclude that $\bmu_j^x=\bmu_j^z$ for every $j\in[0,w]\setminus[a,b]$.
This completes the proof.
<!-- end ieeeproof-30 -->
<!-- proposition-5: body_md -->
\label{prop_labelrecovery}
Let $n\ge T_{\ast}$ and $\bmx,\bmz\in\Sigma_q^n$ satisfy
$\bm{\chi}_k(\bmx)=\bm{\chi}_k(\bmz)$. Denote their respective segments by $\bmu_0^x,\ldots,\bmu_w^x$ and
$\bmu_0^z,\ldots,\bmu_w^z$. Suppose that $\cE_{1,k}^{\ne}(\bmx)\cap\cE_{1,k}^{\ne}(\bmz)\ne\emptyset$ and that $\bmG_{r}^{\ne}(\bmx)=\bmG_{r}^{\ne}(\bmz)$ for every $r\in[0,2U_{\ast}-1]$. Then $\mathsf{Lab}(\bmu_j^x)=\mathsf{Lab}(\bmu_j^z)$ for every $j\in[0,w]$. 

Consequently, given $\bmy\in\cE_{1,k}^{\ne}(\bmx)$, the marker sequence $\bm{\chi}_k(\bmx)$, and
$(\bmG_{r}^{\ne}(\bmx))_{r=0}^{2U_{\ast}-1}$, all segment labels of $\bmx$ are uniquely determined.
<!-- end proposition-5 -->
<!-- ieeeproof-31: body_md -->
By \Cref{lem_affectedsegments}, there exist integers
$0\le a\le b\le w$ such that $b-a+1\le 2U_{\ast}$ and
$ \bmu_j^x=\bmu_j^z$ for every $j\in[0,w]\setminus[a,b]$.
In particular, we have $\mathsf{Lab}\left( \bmu_j^x \right)=\mathsf{Lab}\left( \bmu_j^z \right)$ for every $j\in[0,w]\setminus[a,b]$.

For every $r\in[0,2U_{\ast}-1]$, the equality
$\bmG_{r}^{\ne}(\bmx)=\bmG_{r}^{\ne}(\bmz)$ and
\eqref{eq_bmGr} imply
$$
 \sum_{\substack{j\in[0,w]\\
                  j\equiv r\pmod{2U_{\ast}}}}
 \left( \mathsf{Lab}(\bmu_j^x)-\mathsf{Lab}(\bmu_j^z) \right)
 \equiv\bm{0}\pmod{q}.
$$
All vector congruences in this proof are componentwise.
The summands with indices outside $[a,b]$ are zero.
Therefore,
$$
 \sum_{\substack{j\in[a,b]\\
                  j\equiv r\pmod{2U_{\ast}}}}
 \left( \mathsf{Lab}(\bmu_j^x)-\mathsf{Lab}(\bmu_j^z) \right)
 \equiv\bm{0}\pmod{q}
$$
for every $r\in[0,2U_{\ast}-1]$. This implies $\mathsf{Lab}\left( \bmu_j^x \right)\equiv\mathsf{Lab}\left( \bmu_j^z \right)\pmod{q}$ for every $j\in[a,b]$, since $ \left|[a,b]\right|\le 2U_{\ast}$. Recall that $\mathsf{Lab}\left( \bmu_j^x \right),\mathsf{Lab}\left( \bmu_j^z \right)\in\Sigma_q^{2B_{\ast}}$. Now we conclude that $\mathsf{Lab}\left( \bmu_j^x \right)=\mathsf{Lab}\left( \bmu_j^z \right)$ for every $j$. Now the proof is completed.
<!-- end ieeeproof-31 -->
<!-- proposition-6: body_md -->
\label{prop_complexitySynne}
    For $n\ge1$ and any $\bmx\in\Sigma_q^n$, the syndrome $\mathsf{Syn}_{n}^{\ne}(\bmx)$ can be computed in $O_{q,k}\left( n\log n \right)$ bit operations.
<!-- end proposition-6 -->
<!-- theorem-5: body_md -->
\label{thm_uneqfinalcode}
For $n\ge T_{\ast}$ and $\theta\in\cV_{\mathsf{Syn}}$, every nonempty $\cC^{\ne}(n;\theta)$ is an unequal-length $k$-substring edit correcting code. For any $\bmx\in\cC^{\ne}(n;\theta)$ and any $\bmy\in\cE_{1,k}^{\ne}(\bmx)$, we can recover $\bmx$ from $\bmy$ in $O_{q,k}\left( n^2\log n \right)$ bit operations by brute force. Moreover, there exists some $\theta$ such that $\mathsf{red}_2\left( \cC^{\ne}(n;\theta) \right)\le\log n+O_{q,k}(1)$.
<!-- end theorem-5 -->
<!-- ieeeproof-32: body_md -->
Suppose $\bmx,\bmz\in\cC^{\ne}(n;\theta)$ and
$\bmy\in\cE_{1,k}^{\ne}(\bmx)\cap\cE_{1,k}^{\ne}(\bmz)$. We will prove $\bmx=\bmz$. 

Put $h=n- \left|\bmy\right|$. Then $1\le \left|h\right|\le k$. By \Cref{lem_markerproperty,lem_inducedThburst}, the sequence $\bm{\chi}_k(\bmy)$ is $k$-sparse and is obtained from both $\bm{\chi}_k(\bmx)$ and $\bm{\chi}_k(\bmz)$ by $(T_{\ast},T_{\ast}-h)$-burst errors. \Cref{eq_fullsyndrome} gives $\mathsf{H}_{n}^{\ne}(\bm{\chi}_k(\bmx))=\mathsf{H}_{n}^{\ne}(\bm{\chi}_k(\bmz))$. By \Cref{prop_markerrecovery}, we have $\bm{\chi}_k(\bmx)=\bm{\chi}_k(\bmz)$. Then it follows from \Cref{prop_labelrecovery} that $\mathsf{Lab}(\bmu_j^x)=\mathsf{Lab}(\bmu_j^z)$ for every $j\in\left[ 0,\mathsf{wt}_{L_1}\left(\bm{\chi}_k(\bmx)\right) \right]$, since $\bmG_{r}^{\ne}(\bmx)=\bmG_{r}^{\ne}(\bmz)$ for every $r\in[0,2U_{\ast}-1]$ by \eqref{eq_fullsyndrome}. Notice that for each $j\in\left[ 0,\mathsf{wt}_{L_1}\left(\bm{\chi}_k(\bmx)\right) \right]$, the lengths of $\bmu_j^x$ and $\bmu_j^z$ are determined by $\bm{\chi}_k(\bmx)$. Now the equality $\bmx=\bmz$ follows from \Cref{lem_label}.

We next give the decoding procedure and bound its complexity. Write $m= \left|\bmy\right|=n-h$ and define
$\cT_h=\{t\in[0,k]:0\le t+h\le k\}$. For every $t\in\cT_h$, every $p\in[1,m-t+1]$, and every
$\bmv\in\Sigma_q^{t+h}$, form the candidate $\bmz=\bmy_{[1,p-1]}\bmv\bmy_{[p+t,m]}$. Each candidate has length $m-t+(t+h)=n$. Every length-$n$ sequence that can give $\bmy$ by a $k$-substring edit occurs among these candidates. In particular, the transmitted codeword $\bmx$ occurs in this list.

Since $m\le n+k$, the number of candidates is at most
\begin{equation*}
    \sum_{t\in\cT_h}(m-t+1)q^{t+h}
    \le (m+1)\sum_{s=0}^{k}q^s
    =O_{q,k}(n).
\end{equation*}
Writing one candidate costs
$O_{q,k}(n)$ bit operations. By
\Cref{prop_complexitySynne}, computing the syndrome $\mathsf{Syn}_n^{\ne}$ of each candidate
costs $O_{q,k}\left( n\log n \right)$ bit operations.
The syndrome has $O_{q,k}(\log n)$ bits, so
comparing it with $\theta$ costs at most that many
bit operations. The decoder returns the first candidate whose syndrome equals $\theta$. Such a candidate exists, and the correction property proved above ensures that it is $\bmx$. The total decoding cost is therefore $O_{q,k}\left( n^2\log n \right)$ bit operations.

Finally, we give the redundancy bound. By the pigeonhole principle, there exists $\theta\in\cV_{\mathsf{Syn}}$ such that
$$
 \left|\cC^{\ne}(n;\theta)\right|\ge\frac{q^n}{2U_{\ast}(4U_{\ast})^{K_{\ast}}q^{4B_{\ast}U_{\ast}}M_{n,\ast}},
$$
or equivalently,
\begin{align*}
    \mathsf{red}_2\left( \cC^{\ne}(n;\theta) \right)&\le\log\left( M_{n,\ast} \right)+\log\left( 2U_{\ast}(4U_{\ast})^{K_{\ast}}q^{4B_{\ast}U_{\ast}} \right)\\
    &=\log n+O_{q,k}(1).
\end{align*}
This completes the proof.
<!-- end ieeeproof-32 -->
<!-- example-6: body_md -->
For $k=2$, the parameters in \eqref{eq_uneqpara1} and \eqref{eq_uneqpara2} are $\ell_{\ast}=2$, $B_{\ast}=2q^2(q-1)+1$, $T_{\ast}=8$, $U_{\ast}=10$, $E_{\ast}=182$, $K_{\ast}=558$ and $M_{n,\ast}=12n+182$.
\Cref{thm_uneqfinalcode} gives a code with redundancy at most
$\log(12n+182)+\log20+558\log40+40B_{\ast}\log q$.
<!-- end example-6 -->
<!-- corollary-2: body_md -->
\label{uneq_cor_deletion}
For fixed $q\ge2$ and $k\ge t\ge1$, \Cref{thm_uneqfinalcode} gives a $(\le k)$-burst-deletion correcting code with redundancy at most $\log n+O_{q,k}(1)$ and a $(t,k)$-localized deletion correcting code with redundancy at most $\log n+O_{q,t,k}(1)$.
<!-- end corollary-2 -->
<!-- ieeeproof-33: body_md -->
For fixed $q,k$, the parameters
$\ell_{\ast},D_{\ast},B_{\ast},T_{\ast},U_{\ast},E_{\ast}$,
and $K_{\ast}$ are constants, whereas
$M_{n,\ast}=\Theta_k(n)$. We compute the marker sequence,
the marker syndrome $\mathsf{H}_{n}^{\ne}$, and the label checks as follows.

First, compute $\bmalpha=\bm{\chi}_k(\bmx)$.
Pad $\bmx$ with $k$ zeros on the left and
$\ell_{\ast}+k$ zeros on the right. Scan the resulting sequence using a sliding window of length $\ell_{\ast}+2k+1$. Its $n$ successive windows correspond to $\bmx_{[i-k,i+\ell_{\ast}+k]}$ for $i\in[n]$, under the zero extension.

By \Cref{lem_markerproperty}~(iii), each window determines whether its corresponding coordinate is a marker. Perform the required tests and comparisons, and output $1$ if the coordinate is a marker and $0$ otherwise. Advance the window by removing its first symbol and appending the next symbol. Since $q,k$ are fixed, updating one window and deciding whether the corresponding coordinate is a marker cost $O_{q,k}(1)$ bit operations. There are exactly $n$ windows, so computing $\bmalpha$ costs $O_{q,k}(n)$ bit operations.

Next, compute $\mathsf{H}_{n}^{\ne}(\bmalpha)$ by scanning $\bmalpha$ from left to right. For the VT residue, maintain $V\in[0,M_{n,\ast}-1]$, initially zero, and a position counter $i$, initially one. Whenever $\alpha_i=1$, add $i$ to $V$ and subtract $M_{n,\ast}$ if the sum is at least $M_{n,\ast}$. Since $i\le n<M_{n,\ast}$, the sum is less than $2M_{n,\ast}$. Therefore, at most one subtraction is needed. After reading each bit, increase $i$ by one. The counter $i$, the residue $V$, and the temporary sum have $O_k(\log n)$ bits. Each increment, addition, comparison, or subtraction therefore costs $O_k(\log n)$ bit operations. There are $n$ increments and at most $n$ residue updates. Therefore, computing $\mathsf{VT}\left( \bmalpha \right)\bmod{M_{n,\ast}}$ costs $O_k\left( n\log n \right)$ bit operations.

The $2K_{\ast}+1$ residues $W:=\mathsf{wt}_{L_1}\left(\bmalpha\right)\bmod 2U_{\ast}$, $P_r$, and $R_r$ are computed as we read $\bmalpha$ from left to right. Use $a=i\bmod K_{\ast}$ and $b=i\bmod 2U_{\ast}$ for the current position $i$. Let $c$ be the number of ones already read modulo $K_{\ast}$. Initially, set $a=b=1$, $c=W=0$, and all $P_r,R_r$ to zero. If $\alpha_i=1$, first set $c=(c+1)\bmod K_{\ast}$. Then update $W\gets(W+1)\bmod 2U_{\ast}$, $P_a\gets(P_a+1)\bmod 2$, and
$R_c\gets(R_c+b)\bmod 2U_{\ast}$. For the $j$-th one, the updated value of $c$ is $j\bmod K_{\ast}$, so its position contributes to the required $R_r$. After each bit, increase $a$ modulo $K_{\ast}$ and $b$ modulo $2U_{\ast}$. These counters and residues have $O_k(1)$ bits, and each input bit requires only a constant number
of updates. Thus the computation of these $2K_{\ast}+1$ residues costs $O_k(n)$ bit operations. Together with the VT computation, computing $\mathsf{H}_{n}^{\ne}(\bmalpha)$ costs $O_k\left( n\log n \right)$ bit operations.

Finally, compute the $2U_{\ast}$ label syndromes $\bmG_{r}^{\ne}(\bmx)$ by scanning $0\bmx$ and $\bmalpha$ in order. Initialize all group sums $\bmG_{r}^{\ne}(\bmx)$ to zero and a group counter $g$ to zero. Start the first segment with the initial symbol $0$. Whenever $\alpha_i=1$, finish the current segment before $x_i$, add its label to $\bmG_g^{\ne}(\bmx)$ componentwise modulo $q$, increase $g$ modulo $2U_{\ast}$, and start the next segment at $x_i$. After reading $x_n$, finish the last segment and add its label to the corresponding $\bmG_{r}^{\ne}(\bmx)$. For segment $\bmu_j^x$, the group counter is $g=j\bmod 2U_{\ast}$, as required.

For each segment, start with $d=0$ and two empty strings $\mathsf{Lab}_1$ and $\mathsf{Lab}_2$. Read the segment from left to right. For each new symbol, set $d=\min\{d+1,B_{\ast}\}$.
Add the symbol to the end of $\mathsf{Lab}_1$ if $ \left|\mathsf{Lab}_1\right|<B_{\ast}$. Also add the symbol to the end of $\mathsf{Lab}_2$. If this makes $\mathsf{Lab}_2$ longer than $B_{\ast}$, remove the first symbol of $\mathsf{Lab}_2$. At the end of a segment of length $m$, we have $d=\min\{m,B_{\ast}\}$, and the two length-$d$ sequences $\mathsf{Lab}_1$ and $\mathsf{Lab}_2$ are respectively the prefix and suffix of that segment.
Add $B_{\ast}-d$ zeros to the end of each of $\mathsf{Lab}_1$ and $\mathsf{Lab}_2$, and then form the label $\mathsf{Lab}=\mathsf{Lab}_1\mathsf{Lab}_2$. Since $q,k$ are fixed, the counter and both strings have $O_{q,k}(1)$ bits. Thus each symbol update and each label computation costs $O_{q,k}(1)$ bit operations.

Adding one label to a group sum requires $2B_{\ast}$
component updates modulo $q$. Each update adds two
integers in $[0,q-1]$ and subtracts $q$ if necessary,
so it costs $O_q(1)$ bit operations. Increasing $g$ modulo $2U_{\ast}$, setting $d=0$, and resetting $\mathsf{Lab}_1$ and $\mathsf{Lab}_2$ to empty sequences also cost $O_{q,k}(1)$ bit operations per segment. Reading $0\bmx$ processes $n+1$ symbols and gives at most $n+1$ segments. Updating $d$ and the two stored strings costs
$O_{q,k}(1)$ bit operations for each input symbol.
After a segment has been read, forming its label,
updating the group sum and counter, and preparing
the stored strings for the next segment cost
$O_{q,k}(1)$ bit operations.
There are $n+1$ input symbols and at most $n+1$
segments, so all label checks cost $O_{q,k}(n)$
bit operations.

Computing $\bmalpha$ and the label syndromes costs
$O_{q,k}(n)$ bit operations, while computing
$\mathsf{H}_{n}^{\ne}(\bmalpha)$ costs
$O_k\left( n\log n \right)$ bit operations.
The total cost is therefore
$O_{q,k}\left( n\log n \right)$, as claimed.
<!-- end ieeeproof-33 -->
<!-- proposition-7: body_md -->
\label{prop_example}
Let $q\ge3$ and $k=2$. At length $n=84$, there is a
syndrome value $\theta\in\cV_{\mathsf{Syn}}$ such that
$\cC^{\ne}(n;\theta)$ contains two distinct sequences
with a common received sequence after one substitution.
<!-- end proposition-7 -->
<!-- ieeeproof-34: body_md -->
Here $\ell_{\ast}=2$ and $U_{\ast}=10$, so the segment
labels are grouped by their indices modulo $2U_{\ast}=20$.
Consider the following three sequences:
\begin{equation*}
    \bmy=(0010)^{21},\qquad
    \bmx=(0020)(0010)^{20},\qquad
    \bmz=(0010)^{20}(0020).
\end{equation*}
Clearly, $\bmx\ne\bmz$. All three sequences have length $n=84$. Replacing the symbol $2$ by $1$ at position $3$ of
$\bmx$, or at position $83$ of $\bmz$, gives $\bmy$.
Thus, $\bmx$ and $\bmz$ have a common received sequence after one substitution.

We next check their markers. In each block $00c0$,
where $c\in\{1,2\}$, only the first and third positions are special. Their corresponding length-three sequences are $00c$ and $c00$. A first position is a marker: every other special position within distance two is a third position, whose corresponding sequence starts with $1$ or $2$, whereas $00c$ starts with $0$. A third position is not a marker because the first position of its own block is at distance two and has a smaller corresponding sequence. There are no special coordinates outside $[84]$, since the first two symbols are zero and the sequences are extended by zeros. Hence the set of markers of each of $\bmx$, $\bmy$ and $\bmz$ is $\left\{1,5,9,\ldots, 81\right\}$, and $\bm{\chi}_k(\bmx)=\bm{\chi}_k(\bmz) =\bm{\chi}_k(\bmy)=(1000)^{21}$. Their marker syndromes in \eqref{eq_synHuneq} are
therefore equal.

The markers split $0\bmx$ and $0\bmz$ into the initial
segment $0$, followed by the $21$ displayed blocks
of length four.
For $\bmx$, segments $1$ and $21$ are $0020$ and $0010$;
for $\bmz$, they are $0010$ and $0020$.
All other segments are equal.
Since $1\equiv21\pmod{20}$, both labels are added to
$\bmG_1^{\ne}$.
For both sequences, this group sum contains one label
for $0020$ and one label for $0010$, so the sums are equal.
Every other group sum is also equal because its segments
are unchanged. Thus
$\bmG_r^{\ne}(\bmx)=\bmG_r^{\ne}(\bmz)$ for every
$r\in[0,19]$.

By \eqref{eq_fullsyndrome}, we have
$\mathsf{Syn}_{84}^{\ne}(\bmx)
=\mathsf{Syn}_{84}^{\ne}(\bmz)$.
Take $\theta$ to be this common value.
Then $\bmx,\bmz\in\cC^{\ne}(84;\theta)$, and both
give $\bmy$ after one substitution, as required.
<!-- end ieeeproof-34 -->
